\title{\textbf{Never Predict Noise}\\[2pt]
\large Manifold-Aligned Prediction as Generative Inference}
\author{Flyxion}
\date{November 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Generative models succeed when their dynamics respect the low-dimensional
structure that organizes empirical data, and they fail when they spend
capacity on directions that carry no such structure. This paper develops
\emph{manifold-aligned generative inference} (MAGI) and separates, as
carefully as it can, what is proved from what is conjectured and what is
offered as interpretation. The proved core is short. A flow keeps a
submanifold invariant exactly when its velocity is tangent; isotropic
noise in $\R^n$ places a fraction $1-d/n$ of its squared norm in the
normal space; and, as the noise scale vanishes, the scaled Jacobians of the optimal clean-data and noise predictors converge to the tangent and normal projectors respectively, so that for direct-output models the two prediction targets differ in effective rank by the gap between $d$ and $n-d$. A second thread concerns the word ``noise'' itself. Whether a
residual is noise is a relative question: it depends on the claim being
tested and on the model class in which the residual is described. A verified counterexample refutes a universal claim deductively, whereas a statistical exception rejects a noise model only at a stated level, and either may expose a missing hypothesis or a family of cases that the original formulation concealed. Tangency governs admissible motion under a specified model, and residual evidence governs whether that model should be kept. We state a code-length criterion relative to an admissible class of extensions and a calibrated test for residual structure. The
remaining sections extend the geometry to inference, contextual
coherence, Morse dynamics, proxy optimization, and several social and
cognitive settings, each labelled according to its evidential status. The
experimental section is a protocol with stated null hypotheses and a
calibration baseline, not a report of results.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}

Modern generative systems operate in spaces of very high dimension. Images
occupy $\R^{H\times W\times 3}$, text occupies a large discrete
vocabulary at every position, and sensorimotor streams are continuous and
multimodal. Empirical data occupy a small, structured subset of these
spaces. The manifold hypothesis states this precisely: natural data lie on
or near a smooth, or piecewise smooth, submanifold $M\subset\R^n$ of
dimension $d\ll n$~\cite{fefferman2016testing}. The surrounding ambient
space has no comparable structure. The central proposal of this paper is
that a predictive system should allocate its predictive capacity along the
tangent directions of $M$ and none along the normal directions, which is
the content of the title.

The slogan is easy to misread, and most of the work below consists of
making it precise. It does not prohibit stochasticity, since variation
along tangent directions is how a model explores the admissible space. It
does not identify rarity with noise, since a sparse region of $M$ is part
of $M$. And it does not make ``noise'' an intrinsic property of an
observation. Whether a residual is noise depends on the claim under test and on the model class used to describe it (Section~\ref{sec:claim}). The thesis therefore has two parts that must be kept apart. Tangency governs admissible motion under a specified model: given $M$, an update should not draw on normal directions. Residual evidence governs whether to retain that model: a normal residual that proves to be structured across observations is information about the adequacy of $M$. The Gaussian perturbation of Section~\ref{sec:geometry}, the residual of a fitted manifold in Section~\ref{sec:claim}, and the conditional noise estimate of Section~\ref{sec:jit} play different mathematical roles, and the title covers all three only in the sense that a model should avoid fitting unpredictable realizations while remaining able to learn the structure of its errors. A verified counterexample refutes a universal claim deductively, which no amount of statistical evidence can do, while a statistical exception rejects a noise model at a stated level, and neither says what should replace the rejected formulation. The practical question is therefore never ``is this noise?'' in the abstract, but ``noise with respect to which claim, and under which model?''

The paper has three layers. The first layer consists of results that are
stated with proofs: the invariance characterization of tangent flows
(Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}), the concentration of noise in the normal
space (Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration}), the exact Jacobian identities and small-noise limits of the optimal clean and noise predictors (Propositions~\ref{prop:rankgap} and~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit}), and a conservative rejection bound for a prespecified exception (Proposition~\ref{prop:witness}). The second layer consists of
conjectures with stated tests, chiefly the proposal that the coupling
subspaces of attention heads carry the tangent structure of the data
(Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention}). The third layer is interpretive: the
geometry is offered as a vocabulary for social, institutional and
cognitive phenomena in Sections~\ref{sec:failure} and~\ref{sec:applications}, and
no theorem is claimed there.

\section{Geometry of the Ambient Space}\label{sec:geometry}

\subsection{Manifolds, tangent and normal spaces}

Let $M\subset\R^n$ be a smooth embedded submanifold of dimension $d$. For
$x\in M$ the tangent space $\TM{x}$ is the set of velocities of smooth
curves in $M$ through $x$, and the normal space is
$\NM{x}=(\TM{x})^\perp$, so that $\R^n=\TM{x}\oplus\NM{x}$ orthogonally.
We write $P_T(x)$ and $P_N(x)=I-P_T(x)$ for the orthogonal projectors and
$\Proj_{N}$ for the normal component of a vector. The \emph{reach}
$\tau=\reach(M)$ is the largest $r$ such that every point within distance
$r$ of $M$ has a unique nearest point $\Pi_M(x)\in M$~\cite{federer1959curvature}.
We assume $\tau>0$ throughout, which holds for compact $C^2$ submanifolds.

surface patch. The patch is the graph of a smooth function in a
$2+1$ dimensional schematic, and the particular vector $\varepsilon$ is one
illustrative draw. It splits into $P_T\varepsilon$ in the tangent plane and
$P_N\varepsilon$ along the normal line. For an isotropic perturbation in
dimension $n$ the squared-norm fraction in the tangent space has mean $d/n$,
which is $2/3$ in this drawing and small when $d\ll n$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration}). The same view and the same colors for
tangent and normal displacement are used in every figure.}
\label{fig:decomposition}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Admissibility-induced geometry}

Positing $M$ as a given object invites the objection that lawful
structure has been assumed rather than derived. A more defensible
position treats manifold structure as emergent from constraints on
allowable transformations. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a space of possible
histories or configurations and let $\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}$ be the
subset satisfying three constraints at once: compatibility of successive
states under the governing dynamics, conservation of designated
invariants under transport, and coherence across overlapping contexts
(Section~\ref{sec:coherence}). The working definition of the semantic
manifold is a connected component of the admissible set,
$M:=\mathrm{Conn}(\mathcal{A})$, taken under the assumption that this
component is a smooth embedded submanifold, or a Whitney stratified space
when it is not. Tangent directions are then the directions along which
admissible deformation exists, and normal directions are those blocked by
constraint violation. This definition does not prove that any given
dataset has such structure. It records what ``lawful'' is taken to mean
when the word is used below, and it makes explicit that the manifold
hypothesis is an empirical hypothesis about $\mathcal{A}$ and not an
a priori fact.

\subsection{Noise is almost entirely normal}

The following lemma is the quantitative core of the phrase ``noise lives
in the normal bundle.''

\begin{lemma}[Concentration of normal mass]\label{lem:concentration}
Let $T\subset\R^n$ be a $d$-dimensional subspace and let
$\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_n)$. Then
$\|P_T\varepsilon\|^2/\|\varepsilon\|^2\sim\mathrm{Beta}(d/2,(n-d)/2)$,
independent of $\|\varepsilon\|$, with mean $d/n$ and variance
$2d(n-d)/\bigl(n^2(n+2)\bigr)$. In particular the normal fraction
$\|P_N\varepsilon\|^2/\|\varepsilon\|^2$ has mean $1-d/n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose an orthonormal basis adapted to $T\oplus T^\perp$. By rotation
invariance of the Gaussian, $\|P_T\varepsilon\|^2\sim\chi^2_d$ and
$\|P_N\varepsilon\|^2\sim\chi^2_{n-d}$ are independent. The ratio
$A/(A+B)$ of independent $\chi^2_d$ and $\chi^2_{n-d}$ variables is
$\mathrm{Beta}(d/2,(n-d)/2)$, and is independent of $A+B=\|\varepsilon\|^2$.
The mean and variance are those of the Beta distribution.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
For $n=256\cdot256\cdot3=196{,}608$ and intrinsic dimension $d$ on the
order of tens to low hundreds, which is the range reported by estimators
for natural image data~\cite{pope2021intrinsic}, the expected tangent
fraction of isotropic noise is below $10^{-3}$. A Monte Carlo check with
$n=2000$, $d=20$ and $3000$ samples gave a mean tangent fraction of
$0.00996$ against the theoretical $0.01$ and a variance of
$9.7\times10^{-6}$ against $9.9\times10^{-6}$. The original statement that
the noise vector \emph{lies in} $\NM{x}$ is false for isotropic noise. The
correct statement is that all but a fraction $d/n$ of its squared norm
does, and this is what the rest of the paper uses.
\end{remark}

\section{The No-Noise Criterion}

\subsection{Tangency is invariance}

The central criterion has a precise and non-tautological form. Let
$f$ be a locally Lipschitz vector field on a neighborhood of a closed
embedded $C^1$ submanifold $M$ and consider $\dot x=f(x)$.

\begin{theorem}[Invariance characterization]\label{thm:invariance}
The submanifold $M$ is invariant under the flow of $f$ (every solution
that starts in $M$ stays in $M$ for as long as it exists, forward and
backward in time) if and only if $f(x)\in\TM{x}$ for every $x\in M$.
Equivalently, $\Proj_{\NM{x}}f(x)=0$ on $M$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
($\Leftarrow$) If $f$ is tangent on $M$, then $f|_M$ is a locally
Lipschitz vector field on the manifold $M$, so in any chart the
Picard--Lindel\"of theorem gives an integral curve contained in $M$. Its
image is a solution of the ambient equation, and by uniqueness of
ambient solutions it is the solution. Applying the same argument to $-f$,
which is also tangent, gives backward invariance. ($\Rightarrow$) If the
solution through $x\in M$ stays in $M$, it is a $C^1$ curve in $M$ whose
velocity at time zero is $f(x)$, so $f(x)\in\TM{x}$.
\end{proof}

This is the Nagumo--Brezis invariance theorem specialized to
submanifolds~\cite{nagumo1942lage,brezis1970characterization}, whose
tangent cone is the tangent space. It is the exact sense in which
``explanation is tangent and hallucination is normal'': a dynamics with a
normal component $\Proj_N f(x)\ne0$ at some point of $M$ leaves $M$ at
first order.

\begin{lemma}[Exit from the manifold]\label{lem:exit}
Let $x\in M$ and $v\in\R^n$. Then $\dist(x+\eta v,M)=\eta\|\Proj_{\NM{x}}v\|+O(\eta^2)$
as $\eta\to0^+$. If $v\in\TM{x}$ then $\dist(x+\eta v,M)=O(\eta^2)$, with a
constant depending on $\|v\|$ and on the curvature of $M$ near $x$. If $M$ is
an affine subspace, a tangent step stays on $M$ exactly.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first statement expresses the directional derivative of the distance
function to a $C^2$ submanifold at a point of the manifold. For the second,
choose a $C^2$ curve $c$ in $M$ with $c(0)=x$ and $c'(0)=v$, for instance in
a chart. Taylor expansion gives $\|x+\eta v-c(\eta)\|=O(\eta^2)$, so
$\dist(x+\eta v,M)\le\|x+\eta v-c(\eta)\|=O(\eta^2)$. For an affine subspace
the line $x+\eta v$ lies in $M$.
\end{proof}

A discrete tangent step can therefore leave $M$ at second order, a step with a
normal component leaves at first order, and on an affine subspace a tangent
step does not leave at all. This is why every discrete tangent update in
Appendix~\ref{app:methods} is followed by a retraction $R_x(v)=\Pi_M(x+v)$.

\caption{Exact construction on the unit sphere with step size $h=1$ and a
unit tangent vector $v$ at $x$. Left, the tangent plane at $x$ contains the
trial step, and its endpoint $x+hv$ lies outside the sphere. Right, the
cross-section by the plane through the center containing $x$ and $v$. The
endpoint is at distance $\sqrt{1+h^2}\approx1.414$ from the center, so it is
$\sqrt{1+h^2}-1\approx0.41$ from the sphere, which is $h^2/2$ to leading
order. The retraction $\Pi_M$ moves it along the radial line to the sphere,
and the displacement from $x$ to $\Pi_M(x+hv)$ is curved in the ambient
space.}
\label{fig:euler}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Generators and Jacobian rank}

\begin{proposition}[Generator tangency]\label{prop:generator}
Let $G:Z\to\R^n$ be $C^1$ with $G(Z)\subset M$. Then
$\mathrm{Range}\,DG(z)\subset T_{G(z)}M$ for every $z$. Conversely, if
$G$ has constant rank $r$ and $r>d$, then $G(Z)\not\subset M$, and the
image is locally an $r$-dimensional submanifold of Hausdorff dimension $r>d$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a $C^1$ curve $z(t)$, the curve $G(z(t))$ lies in $M$, so its
velocity $DG(z)\dot z$ lies in $T_{G(z)}M$. For the converse, the
constant rank theorem makes $G(Z)$ locally an $r$-dimensional embedded
submanifold, which cannot be contained in a $d$-manifold when $r>d$.
\end{proof}

This replaces the earlier ``off-manifold catastrophe'' statement, which
asserted a dimension increase without the constant-rank hypothesis that
makes it true. The proposition says that a generator which stays on $M$
has Jacobian range inside the tangent bundle, and that a generator whose
Jacobian has more directions than $M$ has cannot stay on it.

\subsection{Tangent noise and normal noise}

The criterion should not be read as a prohibition on variance. Decompose a
perturbation as $\eta=\eta_T+\eta_N$ with $\eta_T\in\TM{x}$ and
$\eta_N\in\NM{x}$. The tangent component is structured variation along
directions the manifold supports. It drives exploration, allows
variational inference to avoid degenerate fixed points, and corresponds to
the controlled variability that underlies learning in biological systems.
The normal component carries no persistent information under the current model, and the qualification matters: a normal residual that is structured across observations is evidence that the model is inadequate (Section~\ref{sec:claim}). To
``never predict noise'' is to refrain from allocating predictive capacity
to directions in $\NM{x}$, and it does not require eliminating uncertainty
or collapsing a distribution to a point mass. The invariance criterion constrains the \emph{drift} of the dynamics; it does not constrain the diffusion along $M$.

Three roles of normal variation should be distinguished. The isotropic perturbation of Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration} is a generative assumption about how observations are produced. The residual of a fitted manifold is data for criticizing the model, and is treated in Section~\ref{sec:claim}. The conditional noise estimate $\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\mid x_t]$ of Section~\ref{sec:jit} is a learning target. Tangency is a rule for admissible motion once the model is held fixed, and it does not decide whether the model should be held fixed.

\section{What Is Called Noise, and With Respect to Which Claim}\label{sec:claim}

The geometric account so far treats the manifold $M$ as fixed. In
practice $M$ is a hypothesis, and the same observation may be noise
relative to one hypothesis and evidence relative to another. This section
makes that dependence explicit. It also separates the two roles a
residual can play: as a case to be forecast, and as a test of a rule.

\subsection{Forecast regime and test regime}

Consider $N$ cases generated by an unknown rule and a model $\hat p$ fitted to
them. Three roles of an exceptional case must be separated. As a test of a
universal claim $\forall x\,\varphi(x)$, a verified case $x_0$ with
$\neg\varphi(x_0)$ refutes the claim deductively, and the refutation does not
depend on how many cases agreed with it. As evidence against a probabilistic
model, an observation can only reject the model at a stated level, since a
Gaussian, for example, has unbounded support and no single observation is
impossible under it. As a contribution to a forecast, one case has weight
$1/N$ in a fixed empirical average, which is why a single exception tells a
forecaster little about the next case. That weight is not a bound on
influence: when the case introduces a previously unseen regime or has high
leverage, its effect on a refitted model can be much larger than $1/N$.

\begin{proposition}[Rejection of a noise model]\label{prop:witness}
Suppose $x=y+\sigma g$ with $y\in M$ and $g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_n)$. For a
prespecified observation and every $t>0$,
\[
\Pr\bigl[\dist(x,M)>\sigma(\sqrt n+t)\bigr]\le e^{-t^2/2}.
\]
If instead the observation is selected as the one with the largest distance
among $N$ independent cases, then
$\Pr[\max_i\dist(x_i,M)>\sigma(\sqrt n+t)]\le N e^{-t^2/2}$, so $t$ must be
of order $\sqrt{2\log N}$ or larger before rejection at a given level.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $y\in M$, $\dist(x,M)\le\|x-y\|=\sigma\|g\|$. The map $g\mapsto\|g\|$ is
$1$-Lipschitz, so by Gaussian concentration
$\Pr[\|g\|>\mathbb{E}\|g\|+t]\le e^{-t^2/2}$, and $\mathbb{E}\|g\|\le\sqrt n$.
The selected-maximum statement follows from a union bound over the $N$ cases.
\end{proof}

The bound is a level-controlled statistical rejection under the stated
Gaussian model. It is valid for a prespecified observation, with the
adjustment above for selection, and it is conservative, since it ignores the
fact that the tangent part of $g$ moves $x$ along $M$ and not away from it. It
should not be confused with the deductive refutation of a universal statement
by a verified counterexample. A caution applies in the other direction. A run
of counterexamples found by a search procedure measures the tractability of
the problem under that search regime as much as it measures the falsity of
the claims searched. When counterexamples are cheap to find and proofs are
expensive, a high rate of counterexamples among attempted conjectures
reflects selection by the search and not the base rate of false conjectures.
The existence of a witness settles the negative case, and the positive case
requires an argument over an entire domain.

\subsection{Three things a model can learn}

A model that improves its predictions has done one of three things. It may
have learned a genuine regularity, it may have fitted an accident of the
sample, or it may have discovered that the original target grouped
together cases governed by different rules. The third case is the one
that most often looks like noise from inside the original formulation.
Suppose the true conditional law is a mixture
$p(y\mid x)=\sum_k\pi_k\,p_k(y\mid x)$ over latent regimes $k$, and a
single-rule model $q(y\mid x)$ is fitted. The residuals of $q$ are then
structured: conditional on the regime, they have nonzero mean, and they
are predictable from any feature correlated with $k$. This is the
structure behind Simpson-type reversals, and it is the sense in which
stratified models, such as the Whitney stratified spaces of
Appendix~\ref{app:background}, arise. An exception that sits on a
lower-dimensional stratum is a counterexample to a smoothness claim on the
whole and an expected feature of the stratified formulation.

\subsection{A code-length criterion}

The distinction between noise and structure can be made operational by
comparing description lengths in the sense of Rissanen~\cite{rissanen1978modeling}.
The comparison is relative to two choices that must be stated: a family of
admissible extensions and a coding scheme.

\begin{definition}[Structure relative to a model class]\label{def:structure}
Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a dataset, $\mathcal{H}$ a model class with best total
code length $\ell(\mathcal{H})=L(\mathcal{H})+L(\mathcal{D}\mid\mathcal{H})$,
and $\mathcal{E}$ a specified family of admissible extensions
$\mathcal{H}'\supset\mathcal{H}$, for example additional tangent directions,
latent regimes or strata, with a specified coding scheme that assigns
$L(\mathcal{H}')$. The residuals of the best model in $\mathcal{H}$ are
\emph{noise relative to $(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E})$} if no
$\mathcal{H}'\in\mathcal{E}$ satisfies $\ell(\mathcal{H}')<\ell(\mathcal{H})$,
and \emph{structure relative to $(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E})$} if some
$\mathcal{H}'\in\mathcal{E}$ does.
\end{definition}

The cost of the extension is charged once, inside $L(\mathcal{H}')$. The
criterion is relative in exactly the two respects the definition names: what
extensions are admitted, and how they are coded. A residual that is noise
for one family of extensions may be structure for a richer one.

In the manifold setting a spectral diagnostic is available. Suppose the
residuals of the fitted $d$-dimensional manifold are expressed in a fixed
common normal space of dimension $q=n-d$ and, under the null, are isotropic
with variance $\sigma^2$. The eigenvalues of their sample covariance over $N$
samples then follow the Marchenko--Pastur law~\cite{marchenko1967distribution}
with aspect ratio $q/N$, and as $q,N\to\infty$ with $q/N\to\gamma$ the largest
eigenvalue concentrates at $\sigma^2(1+\sqrt\gamma)^2$. This edge is an
asymptotic reference point and not a finite-sample rejection threshold. An
eigenvalue clearly above it shows that the residuals depart from the isotropic
null. A spiked-covariance signal above the Baik--Ben Arous--P\'ech\'e
threshold~\cite{baik2005phase} is one explanation, namely an omitted tangent
direction. It is not the only one: anisotropic measurement error, curvature
that the fit has not captured, and error in the estimated tangent spaces also
produce outliers. When the normal spaces vary across observations, the
residual coordinates need a common frame, or the test needs a null model that
reproduces the whole fitting and projection procedure, for instance by
parametric bootstrap. The result is a calibrated test for residual structure,
and the planted-direction experiment of Section~\ref{sec:protocol} measures
when the procedure can recover a missing degree of freedom.

\subsection{Search and reformulation}

The interview with Borcherds and Weinstein that motivates part of this
paper~\cite{borcherds2026conversation} turns on a distinction between
problem solving and theory building, and the present section gives that
distinction a place in the geometry. Finding an exception to a conjecture
is a search problem: the target is fixed, the success condition is
recognizable, and a witness can be checked. Finding a formulation in which
the exception is expected changes the target space. In the language above,
the first operation lowers the loss within $\mathcal{H}$, while the
second replaces $\mathcal{H}$ by a class $\mathcal{H}'$ that contains a
stratum, a latent regime or an additional tangent direction in which the
exception is unremarkable. Machine search is well suited to the first
operation. The second requires deciding which objects to define and which
hypotheses to vary, decisions that a named conjecture has already made on
the solver's behalf. The follow-up question to ask of any verified exception is therefore whether it exposes a missing hypothesis, or points to a family of cases that the original formulation concealed. Finding a witness settles a specified conjecture, and finding the formulation under which the witness becomes intelligible is a further mathematical achievement.

The interpretation of dark energy provides a cosmological instance of this
distinction. In their conversation on mathematics and artificial
intelligence, Borcherds and Weinstein~\cite{borcherds2026conversation} turn
from problems with specified solutions to questions for which even the form
of an adequate answer remains open. Weinstein suggests that the cosmological
constant is almost certainly the vacuum expectation value of a field. The
suggestion brings together two questions with different evidential
requirements. The first is whether a constant term in the gravitational
equations adequately describes the observed expansion. The second is what
physical mechanism accounts for that term, or for the apparent departure from
it.

A reproducible discrepancy in the expansion history can place pressure on the
constant model even when the data leave several explanations open. A rolling
scalar field, a change in the gravitational dynamics, and a systematic error
in the observations can each produce residual structure under the original
fit. The residual becomes evidence for a particular mechanism when that
mechanism predicts some further relation that survives an independent test.
In this case, the work of theory building consists in specifying what else
should be observed if the proposed explanation is correct. The distinction is
the same one that governs the revision of a fitted manifold. A departure can
establish where the existing description loses adequacy while leaving the
geometry of its replacement underdetermined.

\section{Clean Prediction and Noise Prediction}\label{sec:jit}

Li and He report that diffusion-style models trained to predict the clean
sample perform markedly better than otherwise identical models trained to
predict the noise or a velocity, particularly when patches are large and
the network is narrow relative to the data dimension~\cite{li2025backtobasics}.
They attribute the effect to the manifold hypothesis. This section gives
that attribution a precise form.

\subsection{Exact identities and small-noise limits}

Write the observation as $Y=aX+b\varepsilon$ with $X$ distributed on $M$,
$\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_n)$ independent of $X$, $a=\sqrt{\alpha_t}$ and
$b=\sqrt{1-\alpha_t}$, so that $a^2+b^2=1$. Let
$f_x^\ast(y)=\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]$ be the optimal clean predictor and
$f_\varepsilon^\ast(y)=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\mid Y=y]=(y-a f_x^\ast(y))/b$ the
optimal noise predictor. The Gaussian observation model gives the Jacobians
exactly.

\begin{proposition}[Exact Jacobian identities]\label{prop:rankgap}
Assume $X$ has finite second moments and let $\mathrm{Cov}(X\mid Y=y)$ be the
posterior covariance. Then $f_x^\ast$ is smooth and
\[
Df_x^\ast(y)=\frac{a}{b^2}\,\mathrm{Cov}(X\mid Y=y),
\qquad
b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast(y)=I-a\,Df_x^\ast(y).
\]
In particular $a\,Df_x^\ast+b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast=I$ at every $y$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The posterior density is proportional to $w(x,y)=p(x)\exp(-\|y-ax\|^2/2b^2)$,
and $\partial_y\log w=(ax-y)/b^2$. Differentiating
$\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y]=\int xw\,dx/\int w\,dx$ in $y$ gives the posterior
covariance of $X$ with $(aX-y)/b^2$, namely $(a/b^2)\mathrm{Cov}(X\mid Y=y)$,
because the term in $y$ is constant given $Y=y$ and has zero covariance with
$X$. The second identity follows by differentiating
$f_\varepsilon^\ast=(y-af_x^\ast)/b$.
\end{proof}

This is a stronger starting point than differentiating a projection
approximation to the posterior mean, since an approximation to the value of a
function, even a small one, does not control its derivative. It also shows
that the clean Jacobian at finite noise can have full ambient rank. If $X$ is
uniform on a circle, the posterior given any observation has positive density
around the whole circle, so its covariance is positive definite, including in
the normal direction at an observation on the scaled circle. The rank gap is
therefore a statement about a limit of scaled Jacobians and not about the
Jacobians at positive noise.

\begin{proposition}[Small-noise limit]\label{prop:rankgaplimit}
Let $M$ be a compact $C^3$ submanifold and let $X$ have a positive $C^1$
density with respect to the volume measure of $M$. Fix $x\in M$ and evaluate at
$y=ax$. As $b\to0$, with $a=\sqrt{1-b^2}$,
\[
a\,Df_x^\ast(ax)\longrightarrow P_T(x),
\qquad
b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast(ax)\longrightarrow P_N(x).
\]
Consequently the participation ratios of the scaled Jacobians approach $d$ and
$n-d$ respectively.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch]
By Proposition~\ref{prop:rankgap}, $a\,Df_x^\ast=(a^2/b^2)\mathrm{Cov}(X\mid Y=ax)$.
The posterior density of $X$ is proportional to
$p(x')\exp(-a^2\|x'-x\|^2/2b^2)$, so the mass outside any fixed neighborhood of
$x$ in $M$ is exponentially small in $b^{-2}$. In a chart $u\mapsto\varphi(u)$
with $\varphi(0)=x$, put $u=(b/a)w$. Then
$\|\varphi(u)-x\|^2=\|D\varphi(0)u\|^2+O(|u|^3)$ and $p$ is smooth and
positive, so $w$ converges in law to $\mathcal{N}(0,(D\varphi^\top D\varphi)^{-1})$
and $X-x=(b/a)D\varphi(0)w+O(b^2)$. Hence
$\mathrm{Cov}(X\mid Y=ax)=(b^2/a^2)\,D\varphi(D\varphi^\top D\varphi)^{-1}D\varphi^\top+o(b^2)
=(b^2/a^2)P_T+o(b^2)$, and multiplying by $a^2/b^2$ gives the first limit.
The second follows from $b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast=I-a\,Df_x^\ast$. The orthogonal
projectors $P_T$ and $P_N$ have $d$ and $n-d$ unit singular values, which
gives the participation ratios.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Affine case, exact]\label{ex:affine}
Let $X$ be Gaussian on an affine $d$-plane with tangent covariance $\lambda I_d$.
Then $a\,Df_x^\ast=c\,P_T$ and $b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast=P_N+(1-c)P_T$ with
$c=a^2\lambda/(a^2\lambda+b^2)$. Even with zero curvature and infinite reach,
the noise predictor retains a tangent response at positive $b$, and the scale
of variation along the manifold matters as well as the reach.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Scope and architecture]
A bottleneck constrains the Jacobian rank of a map that factors through it. A
direct-output network whose width is below $n-d$ therefore cannot represent
the limiting noise Jacobian $P_N$, whereas a network of width at least $d$ can
represent the limiting clean Jacobian $P_T$. An explicit input skip bypasses
the constraint: a model that computes $(y-af_x(y))/b$ represents the noise
predictor from the same learned clean predictor and a fixed linear path. The
rank gap is therefore a statement about the targets of direct-output models,
and not about every architecture. Protocol H1 compares direct-output models
with a skip-connected control in order to measure how much of the advantage of
clean prediction comes from requiring the learned path to carry the
near-identity normal response. The limits above are evaluated at points on
the scaled manifold. For typical noisy observations, whose normal offset has
size of order $b\sqrt{n-d}$, the dependence of the Jacobian on that offset is
not controlled here, and the statement does not apply uniformly when
$b\sqrt n$ is comparable to the reach. Large $b$ is likewise outside the
scope.
\end{remark}

\caption{Schematic of the small-noise limits in
Proposition~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit}, drawn with $d=2$ and $n=3$. Left, the
scaled clean Jacobian $a\,Df_x^\ast$ acts as $P_T$ on a vector $w$ and retains
the tangent plane. Right, the scaled noise Jacobian $b\,Df_\varepsilon^\ast$
acts as $P_N$ and retains the normal direction. In ambient dimension $n$ the
two retained subspaces have dimensions $d$ and $n-d$, and the participation
ratios of the scaled Jacobians approach those values. The drawing shows the
complementary subspaces only, and the rank comparison in the regime
$n-d\gg d$ is a statement about the limits and not something the picture
displays.}
\label{fig:projectors}
\end{figure}

\begin{remark}[Status of the earlier tangent-flow theorem]
An earlier version of this paper stated that Transformer layers implement
a tangent-flow update $x_{t+1}=x_t-\eta\,\Pi_{T}\nabla\mathcal{L}$ and
justified it by the remark that backpropagation ``automatically enforces
tangent projection.'' That justification does not hold: the gradient of a
loss with respect to parameters has no reason to lie in the tangent space of
the data manifold. The claims that survive are Propositions~\ref{prop:rankgap}
and~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit}, which concern the \emph{targets}, and
Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention}, which concerns the \emph{mechanism}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Patches are a cover without overlaps}

An earlier version also described patching in a Vision Transformer as
learning charts $\phi_i$ stitched by attention into a sheaf with
$\phi_i=\phi_j$ on overlaps. Standard patch embeddings partition the image
into disjoint patches, so there are no overlaps on which chart agreement
could be imposed. Coherence across patches is imposed only through the
attention coupling, and the sheaf language of Section~\ref{sec:coherence}
applies to that coupling and not to the patch geometry.

\section{Near-Orthogonality and Learned Coupling}\label{sec:coupling}

Weinstein states the point informally in the interview: generic vectors in
a large space are nearly orthogonal, and an inner product learned in an
attention head is what makes particular pairs meaningful to each
other~\cite{borcherds2026conversation}. The following results make the
first half exact and the second half a testable conjecture.

\begin{lemma}[Near-orthogonality]\label{lem:nearorth}
If $u,v$ are independent and uniform on $S^{n-1}$, then
$\Pr[|\langle u,v\rangle|\ge t]\le 2e^{-nt^2/2}$ for all $t>0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $v$. The inner product $\langle u,v\rangle$ has the distribution of one
coordinate of a uniform point of $S^{n-1}$, and the measure of a spherical
cap $\{u_1\ge t\}$ is at most $e^{-nt^2/2}$~\cite{ball1997elementary}. The
two-sided bound doubles it.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Packing]\label{cor:packing}
For $0<t<1$ and $m<e^{nt^2/4}$ there exist $m$ unit vectors in $\R^n$ with
all pairwise inner products of absolute value less than $t$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Draw $m$ independent uniform vectors. By Lemma~\ref{lem:nearorth} and a
union bound over $\binom m2$ pairs the probability that some pair has inner
product at least $t$ is at most $m^2e^{-nt^2/2}$, which is less than $1$
when $m<e^{nt^2/4}$.
\end{proof}

The corollary is the geometric content of superposition, in which a network
stores many more nearly orthogonal feature directions than it has
dimensions~\cite{elhage2022superposition}. Orthogonality is the default
state of a high-dimensional representation, and relatedness is the
exception that has to be created.

For attention, the score between a query token $h_i$ and a key token $h_j$ is
$h_i^\top B\,h_j$ with $B=W_Q^\top W_K/\sqrt{d_k}$~\cite{vaswani2017attention}.

\begin{proposition}[Coupling subspace]\label{prop:coupling}
The matrix $B$ has rank at most $d_k$. The attention scores depend on the
residual stream $h\in\R^{d_{\mathrm{model}}}$ only through
$W_Qh$ and $W_Kh$. In particular the component of $h$ in
$\ker W_Q\cap\ker W_K$, a subspace of dimension at least
$d_{\mathrm{model}}-2d_k$, has no effect on any score of that head.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$B$ is a product through a $d_k$-dimensional space. The scores are bilinear
in $W_Qh_i$ and $W_Kh_j$. The kernel statement is the rank-nullity bound
applied to the stacked map $h\mapsto(W_Qh,W_Kh)\in\R^{2d_k}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[Coupling and tangency]\label{conj:attention}
In a trained Transformer the principal subspaces of the coupling forms $B$
across heads and layers align with the tangent spaces of the data
manifold in the residual stream, and the orthogonal complement of the
union of those subspaces carries the normal residual.
\end{conjecture}

The conjecture is falsifiable. Estimate tangent spaces of residual-stream
activations by local principal component analysis~\cite{singer2012vector},
compute the principal angles between those spaces and the row and column
spaces of each $B$, and compare with the chance level
$\sqrt{d/d_{\mathrm{model}}}$ implied by Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration}. The
null hypothesis of no alignment predicts an average tangent fraction at the
chance level. Section~\ref{sec:protocol} gives the protocol. The
conjecture is not established by the arguments in this paper, and the
mechanism should be regarded as a hypothesis about learned coupling and not
as a derived property of attention.

\section{Inference on the Manifold}\label{sec:inference}

\subsection{Active inference as Riemannian gradient flow}

Under the free-energy principle~\cite{friston2010freeenergy,parr2022active}
a system minimizes a variational free energy
$F[q]=\mathbb{E}_q[\log q-\log p(o,z)]$. Suppose the latent variable
$z\in\R^k$ parameterizes a manifold through a decoder $G$ and equip the
latent space with the pullback metric $g_{ij}(z)=\langle\partial_iG,\partial_jG\rangle$.
The gradient flow of $F$ with respect to this metric is
\[
\dot z^i=-g^{ij}(z)\,\partial_jF(z),
\]
a Riemannian gradient flow. It should not be called a geodesic flow:
geodesics are the force-free curves of the metric, whereas this flow follows
the steepest descent of $F$ and reduces to a geodesic only in the absence of
a driving potential. The metric $g=DG^\top DG$ is the pullback of the
Euclidean metric and is not in general the Fisher information metric. The
two coincide in the Gaussian decoder case $p(x\mid z)=\mathcal{N}(G(z),\sigma^2I)$,
where the Fisher metric equals $\sigma^{-2}DG^\top DG$, and in that case the
flow is Amari's natural gradient~\cite{amari1998natural,amari2000methods}.
This is the sense in which tangent-constrained inference and natural-gradient
inference agree: the flow in latent coordinates, pushed forward by $DG$, is a
vector field tangent to $G(Z)$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:generator}.

\subsection{Sparse semantic projection}

Let $E:\R^n\to\R^k$ be an encoder and $G:\R^k\to\R^n$ a decoder with
$G(\R^k)=M$. The semantic projection is $\Pi_{\mathrm{sem}}=G\circ E$. If
$E(x)\in\arg\min_s\|x-G(s)\|$ then $\Pi_{\mathrm{sem}}(x)$ is a nearest point
of $M$ to $x$ and, for $\dist(x,M)<\tau$, equals the unique nearest point
$\Pi_M(x)$~\cite{federer1959curvature}. A linear encoder $E(x)=Wx$ cannot realize this
for a curved $M$ globally, because $\Pi_M$ is nonlinear. It can do so to
first order near a base point, and a few Gauss--Newton iterations in the
latent space (Appendix~\ref{app:methods}) restore the exact projection. A
sparsity penalty on $W$ selects a small number of coordinates through which
the coupling operates, which is the role the coupling subspaces play in
Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention}. The earlier ``consistency theorem'' for
sparse projection reduces to this statement and is not stronger.

\section{Contextual Coherence}\label{sec:coherence}

Perception and cognition are distributed across overlapping contexts such
as visual, linguistic, temporal and proprioceptive ones. Each context
provides partial information about a shared state. Sheaf theory is the
natural language for how local information assembles into a global state,
but it must be used with care, because the sheaf condition by itself
guarantees less than is sometimes claimed.

\subsection{Gluing defects and twisted gluing}

Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a site of contexts and let $\mathcal{F}$ assign to each context
$W$ a set of local semantic states, with restriction maps
$\rho_{WV}:\mathcal{F}(W)\to\mathcal{F}(V)$ for $V\subset W$. If $\mathcal{F}$ is a sheaf, then local
sections that agree exactly on overlaps glue uniquely to a global section.
There is therefore no cohomological obstruction to gluing \emph{strictly
compatible} sections. Two distinct failure modes remain and they must be
distinguished.

The first is \emph{strict incompatibility}: local sections disagree on an
overlap. This is measured by a defect such as
$E_{\mathrm{sheaf}}=\sum_{W,V}\|\rho_{W,W\cap V}(s_W)-\rho_{V,W\cap V}(s_V)\|^2$,
which is zero exactly when the data define a global section.

The second is \emph{twisted gluing}: local sections are meaningful only up
to a gauge. Let $\{W_i\}$ be a cover and let $g_{ij}$ be transition elements
of a sheaf of groups $G$ relating the local descriptions, so that
$s_j=g_{ij}\cdot s_i$ on $W_i\cap W_j$. These data define a global
description only if the cocycle condition $g_{ij}g_{jk}g_{ki}=\mathrm{id}$
holds, and whether the cocycle is a coboundary, which is to say whether a
global gauge exists in which all transitions are trivial, is measured by the
class $[g]\in\check{H}^1(\{W_i\};G)$, a pointed set when $G$ is non-abelian
and a group when $G$ is abelian~\cite{maclane1992sheaves,robinson2014topological}.
A nonzero class is a genuine global obstruction. In the earlier version of
this paper the class $\mathcal{O}\in H^1$ was attached to a sheaf of
sets, for which no such group exists, and ``failure to glue'' was identified
with hallucination. The corrected statement is that strict incompatibility
and nontrivial twisting are two ways in which a collection of locally
plausible states can fail to form a coherent whole, and either may
accompany what is called hallucination without being identical to it.
Contextual structures in physics exhibit the same phenomenon, where local
consistency does not imply global consistency~\cite{abramsky2011sheaf}.

\subsection{Gauge equivalence and fibers}

A representation is a point of a parameter space $\Theta$ and a realization
map $\Phi:\Theta\to\mathcal{F}$ sends it to the semantic function it
computes. The fiber $\Phi^{-1}(f)$ is the set of representations of the same
meaning. Fibers are typically positive dimensional. For a ReLU network each
hidden unit admits a rescaling of incoming and outgoing weights that leaves
the function unchanged, which is a one-parameter gauge group per unit, and
permutations of units give a discrete gauge group. Two representations in the
same fiber are gauge equivalent and carry identical content. Two
representations in different fibers are semantically distinct however close
they are in $\Theta$. This is the sense in which coordinate proximity in
parameter space is not semantic proximity, and it is why gauge-invariant
quantities, not raw weights, are the appropriate objects for comparison.

\section{Dynamics: Potentials, Transport and Curvature}\label{sec:dynamics}

\subsection{Morse potentials and gradient-like cognitive loops}

Let $U:M\to\R$ be a smooth potential. It is a Morse potential when all of its
critical points are nondegenerate. Morse functions are generic, so a small
perturbation of a smooth $U$ is Morse~\cite{milnor1963morse}, and the Morse
property is imposed here as an assumption on $U$ and not derived. The
Riemannian gradient $\nabla_MU$ is tangent to $M$ because $U$ is defined
intrinsically, and the flow $\dot x=-\nabla_MU(x)$ is tangent by
Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}. A discretization
$x_{t+1}=R_{x_t}(-\eta\nabla_MU(x_t))$ with a retraction $R$ is a step of the
cognitive loop which this paper calls CLIO (Cognitive Loop via In-Situ
Optimization).

\begin{definition}[CLIO family]
A CLIO family is a smooth family of endomaps $C_\eta:M\to M$ with
$C_\eta(x)=x+\eta V(x)+O(\eta^2)$, together with a Lyapunov function $L$, such
that the fixed points are the equilibria of $L$; there is strict first-order
descent, meaning that for small $\eta>0$
$L(C_\eta(x))-L(x)\le-\eta\,c(x)$ with $c(x)>0$ off the equilibria; and the
maps commute with a specified group of semantic symmetries.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[CLIO and Lyapunov flows]\label{prop:clio}
(a) Off the equilibria, $\langle\nabla L,V\rangle\le-c(x)<0$, so $V$ is a descent
field for $L$. (b) If $V=-\nabla_MU$ for a Morse potential $U$ and the metric of
$M$, then $C_\eta$ is a first-order discretization of a Morse gradient flow.
(c) If $U$ and the metric are invariant under the symmetry group and the
retraction is equivariant, then the gradient flow is equivariant and so is its
retraction-based discretization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) Expanding $L(C_\eta(x))=L(x)+\eta\langle\nabla L,V\rangle+O(\eta^2)$,
dividing the descent inequality by $\eta$ and letting $\eta\to0$ gives the
claim. (b) is the definition of a gradient flow. (c) The gradient of an
invariant function in an invariant metric is equivariant, and composition with
an equivariant retraction preserves equivariance.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Strict descent for each small $\eta$ does not by itself give first-order
descent. For $L(x)=x^2$ and $C_\eta(x)=(1-\eta^2)x$, $L$ strictly decreases
away from zero while $V=0$, which is why the definition imposes the
first-order inequality. A descent field also need not be a gradient: for a
skew-symmetric $J$ the field $-x+Jx$ is a descent field for $\|x\|^2$ and has
a non-symmetric Jacobian, so it is not the gradient of any function. Gradient
flows are the special case in which the loop has no circulation. The earlier
``CLIO--Morse equivalence'' overlooked both points.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Field interpretation}

In the RSVP (Relativistic Scalar-Vector-Entropy Plenum) picture the dynamics
are described by a scalar field $\Phi$ (scalar capacity), a vector flow
$\mathbf{v}$ and an entropy field $S$. The semantic manifold is modeled as a
level set $\{\Phi=\Phi_0\}$ of a, possibly vector-valued, constraint map at a
regular value, which is a smooth submanifold by the regular value theorem. With
a single scalar field the level set is a hypersurface, and a lower dimension $d$
requires several constraints. It is not a sublevel set, which has nonempty
interior and full dimension. Meaningful dynamics have $\mathbf{v}(x)\in T_xM$,
which is the invariance criterion of Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}, and the
entropy field enters the master functional through the field energy below. The
Morse potential $U$ of the cognitive loop is a function on $M$ assumed to be
built from these fields, and its construction is not specified here. The symbol
$S$ is reserved throughout for the entropy field, and the semantic potential
used for the Morse dynamics is always written $U$, so that the two roles are
not conflated. This is a
modeling convention for connecting the geometry to field language, and no
dynamical law for the fields is derived.

\subsection{Parallel transport and holonomy}

A trajectory can remain tangent at every step and still accumulate
inconsistency through curvature. Let $\gamma:[0,1]\to M$ and let $V(t)$ be a
vector field along $\gamma$. The Levi--Civita connection defines parallel
transport by $\nabla_{\dot\gamma}V=0$, and the quantity
\[
D_\gamma(V)=\int_0^1\|\nabla_{\dot\gamma}V\|^2\,dt
\]
measures how far a given frame departs from parallel transport along the
path. For a closed loop $\gamma$ at $x$, transport defines the holonomy map
$P_\gamma:\TM{x}\to\TM{x}$. If $M$ is flat, $P_\gamma$ is the identity on
loops that are contractible, but a flat manifold with nontrivial topology
can have nontrivial holonomy around non-contractible loops, as the M\"obius
band shows, so flatness alone does not make holonomy vanish. For a small loop
spanning an area element $\omega(X,Y)$ the holonomy deviates from the
identity by the curvature to leading order, and the holonomy algebra at $x$
is generated by curvature operators, which is the content of the
Ambrose--Singer theorem~\cite{ambrose1953holonomy}.

\fill (0.000,0.000,1.000) circle (1.1pt) node[above left=1pt,font=\small] {$N$};
\fill (1.000,0.000,0.000) circle (1.1pt) node[below left=1pt,font=\small] {$A$};
\fill (0.000,1.000,0.000) circle (1.1pt) node[below right=1pt,font=\small] {$B$};
\node[tangcol,font=\scriptsize,below left] at (0.550,0.000,1.000) {$V_0$};
\node[tangcol,font=\scriptsize,below right] at (0.000,0.550,1.000) {$V_1$};
\node[font=\scriptsize,below] at (0.184,0.184,1.000) {$90^\circ$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Exact construction on the unit sphere. The loop runs from the north
pole $N$ down a meridian to $A$ on the equator, along the equator through a
quarter turn to $B$, and back up a meridian to $N$. It encloses an octant of
area $\pi/2$. A tangent vector $V_0$ at $N$ is parallel transported along the
loop. Each leg is a geodesic, along which a parallel vector keeps a constant
angle with the path. It returns as $V_1$ rotated by $90^\circ$, which equals
the enclosed area for Gaussian curvature $1$. The path and the transported
vector are tangent at every point, so the criterion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance} holds throughout, and the rotation is a
holonomy effect.}
\label{fig:holonomy}
\end{figure}

This separates two failure modes that the criterion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance} cannot see. Local drift is a nonzero normal
component at a point. Transport inconsistency is nontrivial holonomy
accumulated along a loop on which every step was tangent. The twisted gluing
class of Section~\ref{sec:coherence} detects discrete incompatibility across
patches, and holonomy detects continuous orientation drift along paths. A
fully coherent system requires both to be trivial.

\subsection{A coherence functional}

Define, for a trajectory $\gamma$ in $M$,
\[
\mathcal{C}(\gamma)=\int_0^1\|\nabla_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma\|^2\,dt+\lambda E_{\mathrm{sheaf}}(\gamma),
\]
where the first term is the integrated squared acceleration, which
vanishes exactly on geodesics, and $E_{\mathrm{sheaf}}(\gamma)$ is the gluing
defect of Section~\ref{sec:coherence} evaluated along the contexts
traversed. Large $\mathcal{C}$ indicates a trajectory that departs from
force-free motion or crosses incompatible contexts. It is a regularizer and
a diagnostic and is not claimed to characterize hallucination exactly.

\subsection{Phase transitions and singularities}

When the metric $g(t)$ of a time-dependent manifold $M_t$ develops curvature
that grows without bound, $\sup_x|Rm(x,t)|\to\infty$, the tangent--normal
decomposition ceases to be defined at the singular points. Geometrically this
corresponds to bifurcation, dimension drop or loss of connectivity, which are
the transitions that a smooth Morse picture cannot describe. Ricci flow
$\partial_tg_{ij}=-2R_{ij}$ is the standard example of a flow that smooths
curvature, and in dimension three with positive Ricci curvature it converges
to a round metric~\cite{hamilton1982three}. In general it forms singularities, and Perelman's
surgery theory handles them by controlled topological modification~\cite{perelman2002entropy}.
The analogy to semantic dynamics, in which a controlled restructuring
replaces a fractured region by a simpler coherent one, is offered as a
formal analogy and not as a claim that semantic manifolds evolve by Ricci
flow.

\section{The Master Functional}

The ingredients above can be combined into a single variational objective,
which is stated here as a definition and not as a theorem:
\begin{align*}
\mathcal{J}={}&F[q(s)]+\lambda_1\|W\|_1+\lambda_2E_{\mathrm{sheaf}}\\
&+\lambda_3\,\dist^2(G(s),M)+\lambda_4\int_M\bigl(\|\nabla\Phi\|^2+\|\mathbf{v}\|^2+S^2\bigr)\,d\mu .
\end{align*}
Its stationarity conditions with respect to $s,W,G,S,\Phi,\mathbf{v}$ are the
formal Euler--Lagrange equations of $\mathcal{J}$. They are not derived or
solved here, and no existence or uniqueness claim is made. The functional
records which constraints a fully aligned system would trade off: free energy,
sparsity of coupling, contextual coherence, proximity to $M$, and field
regularity.

\section{Two Failure Modes at Scale}\label{sec:failure}

Two different pathologies are easily conflated, and the geometric language
is useful precisely because it separates them. Table~\ref{tab:modes}
summarizes the distinctions developed in this section.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption{Failure modes distinguished by the geometry.}\label{tab:modes}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.1cm}p{4.3cm}p{4.9cm}@{}}
\toprule
Failure mode & Geometric signature & Diagnostic \\
\midrule
Normal drift & $\Proj_N f\ne0$; first-order exit (Lemma~\ref{lem:exit}) & normal component of updates; tangent alignment score \\
Tangential collapse & variance along $\TM{x}$ shrinks; mode collapse & rank and spread of tangent covariance \\
Blind tangent directions & $\ker D\pi\cap\TM{x}\ne0$ for a proxy $\pi$ & rank of $D\pi|_{\TM{x}}$ \\
Twisted gluing & nontrivial class in $\check H^1$ & cocycle defect across contexts \\
Holonomy & $P_\gamma\ne\mathrm{id}$ on a tangent loop & transport around closed loops \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Rarity is not noise}

A normal distribution describes concentration of samples around a local
mean and says nothing about moral or semantic ranking. A point in a sparse
region of $M$ is unusual without being unstructured, and a point near the
mode of a distribution is common without being semantically privileged.
The statistical and the geometric are independent coordinates, and
conflating them produces tangential collapse: optimization that treats every
departure from the empirical mode as an error compresses variation along
$M$, which appears as mode collapse in generative models, as rigidity in
cognition, and as normalization pressure in social systems. This is a
failure \emph{within} the tangent bundle, in which admissible variation is
lost, and it is distinct from normal drift. A model that restricts itself to
dense regions is predicting the mode and is not obeying the invariance
criterion in any substantive sense. An earlier version of this paper
described normalization as motion in a normal direction, which is incorrect:
convergence toward a mean is motion along the manifold.

\subsection{Proxy optimization}

Optimization rarely acts on the manifold directly. It acts on proxies:
metrics, scores and compressed summaries. Let $\pi:\R^n\to\R^k$ be a proxy
defined on the ambient state and let $J=F\circ\pi$.

\begin{proposition}[Proxy blindness and leakage]\label{prop:proxy}
(a) If $k<d$, then at every $x\in M$ the restriction $D\pi(x)|_{\TM{x}}$ has a
kernel of dimension at least $d-k$. Motion along these tangent directions
produces no change in the proxy and receives no corrective pressure from
$J$. (b) The gradient $\nabla J=D\pi^\top\nabla F$ lies in $(\ker D\pi)^\perp$,
and its normal component $\Proj_N D\pi^\top\nabla F$ vanishes at $x$ if and only if
$D\pi(x)^\top\nabla F\in\TM{x}$. Unless this tangency holds, the gradient step
leaves $M$ at first order by Lemma~\ref{lem:exit}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) By rank--nullity, $\dim\ker(D\pi|_{\TM{x}})\ge d-\mathrm{rank}\,D\pi\ge d-k$.
(b) The range of $D\pi^\top$ is the orthogonal complement of $\ker D\pi$, and
the normal component is the projection onto $\NM{x}$.
\end{proof}

\caption{Schematic with $n=3$, $d=2$ and a scalar proxy, so $k=1<d$. The
solid and dashed curves are two level sets of the linear proxy
$\pi(y)=g\cdot y$ restricted to the surface. The dashed blue line is a blind
direction in $\ker D\pi(x)\cap\TM{x}$, along which the proxy is unchanged to
first order. The ambient vector $D\pi^\top\nabla F$ is drawn with its tangent
projection and its normal component. The normal component is the leakage of
Proposition~\ref{prop:proxy}, and it coexists with the blind direction.}
\label{fig:proxy}
\end{figure}

The earlier formulation required $\ker D\pi\cap\TM{x}=\{0\}$ for a proxy to be
admissible while also assuming $k<d$, a combination that cannot hold by
part (a), so the criterion as stated could never be satisfied. The corrected
statement identifies two separate harms: an unavoidable blind subspace of
dimension at least $d-k$ inside the tangent bundle, which is where Goodhart-type
drift accumulates because nothing resists it~\cite{manheim2018goodhart}, and
first-order leakage into the normal bundle unless the proxy gradient happens
to be tangent. A proxy is adequate for a given purpose only to the extent that
$k\ge d$ on the directions that matter and its gradient is tangent there. This
supplies a formal reading of recursive proxy construction
$X\to M_1\to M_2\to\cdots$: each stage discards directions, so the blind
subspace grows with depth.

\subsection{Maps and territories}

All cognition projects. The pathology is projection closure, the
identification $\pi(X)\equiv X$, which is always false because maps erase
information. A system that optimizes entirely inside a compressed
representation loses sensitivity to what was discarded. The remedy in the
framework is re-grounding: every projection must preserve recoverable
alignment with the structure it summarizes, and the system must be able to
identify which dimensions have been compressed away.

\section{Interpretive Extensions}\label{sec:applications}

The following paragraphs apply the vocabulary of the preceding sections to
social, cognitive and institutional settings. No theorem is claimed, and each
paragraph is a proposal about how to describe a phenomenon, to be judged by
whether the description organizes evidence better than alternatives.

\paragraph{Social normalization.}
Institutions often evaluate individuals against a statistical center. If the
center is treated as prescriptive, the institution applies pressure toward
the mean, which is tangential collapse in the sense of
Section~\ref{sec:failure}, and it loses information carried by the tails,
which are part of the population manifold. The location of a person relative
to the center also depends on the reference population, so restricting the
sampled contexts early mistakes a local average for a universal one.

\paragraph{Category errors.}
Certain questions combine constraints whose solution sets do not intersect,
such as the unstoppable force and the immovable object, so the question
presupposes a point in an empty set. A fluent answer to such a question is a
smooth trajectory in representation space with no point of the semantic
manifold behind it. As a modelling choice, a factual error can be described
as a wrong point on the manifold and a fluent unsupported answer as a
departure from it. Whether a particular language-model output falls in one
class or the other is an empirical question that this description does not
settle.

\paragraph{Civilizational drift.}
Let $M_{\mathrm{real}}$ be the manifold of physical, biological and social
constraints and $M_{\mathrm{proxy}}$ the manifold of institutional
representations that guide collective optimization. A usable measure of their
divergence at corresponding points is
$\delta(x)=\|P_{T_xM_{\mathrm{proxy}}}-P_{T_xM_{\mathrm{real}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$,
which equals the sine of the largest principal angle between the two tangent
spaces and lies between $0$ and $1$. Drift toward $\delta\to1$ means that
optimization pressure in the proxy manifold has vanishing projection onto the
tangent space of the real one. Self-reinforcement arises when performance is
evaluated on the proxy, so that agents adapted to it displace agents who track
the constraint, which is a selection effect and not a geometric necessity.
Whether $\delta$ can be measured for any real institution is an open problem
listed in Section~\ref{sec:future}.

\paragraph{Exploration and admissible novelty.}
Novelty is admissible when a trajectory stays on $M$ while visiting regions of
low sampling density, and inadmissible when its velocity has a normal
component. Systems that suppress all variance collapse the space of
trajectories into a narrow attractor basin and become brittle, so adaptive
systems retain bounded tangent exploration along saddle connections of the
Morse landscape while holding the normal component at zero.

\paragraph{Explanatory humility.}
Successful navigation of a manifold does not require reconstructing its
geometry, so competence does not imply structural understanding, and fluent
explanations can interpolate through semantic space without tracking any
invariant. A claim to understand should therefore be separated from a
claim to perform.

\paragraph{Identity, language, memory.}
Identity can be described as bounded flow within an attractor basin of a
semantic potential, so that continuity is continuity of the attractor
structure and not of position within it. Language can be described as a
transport map $L:M_A\to M_B$ between the semantic manifolds of speaker and
listener, with communication succeeding when $L_\ast(T_xM_A)\approx T_{L(x)}M_B$.
Memory can be described as persistence of a trajectory under a time-dependent
projection that retains relational invariants and discards surface detail.
Each of these is a redescription, and the framework adds a testable claim
only where it supplies a measure such as the transport fidelity above.

\paragraph{Aesthetics, technology, ethics.}
Aesthetic interest can be placed between collapse to redundancy, in which the
effective dimension of the object's semantic space approaches zero, and
collapse to noise, in which no manifold remains. Externalized cognition
compounds the projection failures of the previous section when each layer of
infrastructure compresses the layer below. In ethics the same vocabulary
permits a description of harm as the introduction of curvature or constraint
violation into a manifold shared by other agents, and benefit as tangent
motion that leaves the shared space more navigable. None of these is a
reduction of the corresponding domain to geometry.

\section{Related Work}

Manifold learning methods such as Isomap~\cite{tenenbaum2000isomap} and
locally linear embedding~\cite{roweis2000lle} established nonlinear
dimensionality reduction, and the testing of the manifold hypothesis has its
own sample-complexity theory~\cite{fefferman2016testing,aamari2019nonasymptotic}.
Diffusion and score-based
models~\cite{ho2020denoising,song2021score} predict noise or scores that, have scaled Jacobians that converge to the normal projector by Proposition~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit},
and flow matching~\cite{lipman2023flow} learns vector fields on the ambient
space. Work on diffusion models and manifolds shows that score fields reveal
the dimension of the data manifold~\cite{stanczuk2022diffusion,pidstrigach2022score},
which is the empirical counterpart of the rank statements above. The
free-energy principle~\cite{friston2010freeenergy} supplies the variational
setting, geometric deep learning~\cite{bronstein2021geometric} the symmetry
vocabulary, and neural ordinary differential equations~\cite{chen2018neuralode}
the continuous-time view. Sheaf methods for contextual
consistency~\cite{abramsky2011sheaf,robinson2014topological} supply the
coherence conditions. What MAGI adds to these literatures is the combination of an
invariance criterion for the dynamics, an explicit relativization of the word
noise to a claim, and a set of corrections to commonly assumed equivalences.

\section{Experimental Protocol}\label{sec:protocol}

This section specifies how the claims of the paper would be tested. It
reports no results. Each hypothesis is stated together with the null it is
meant to reject, and with a calibration baseline derived from
Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration}, so that a reported effect can be compared
with what chance alone would produce.

\paragraph{Metrics.} For a vector field $v$ evaluated on samples from $M$ with
known or estimated tangent projectors, the tangent alignment score is
$\mathrm{TAS}=\mathbb{E}\,\|P_Tv\|/\|v\|$ and the normal misalignment is
$\mathrm{NMC}=\mathbb{E}\,\|P_Nv\|/\|v\|$. For an isotropic random field
$\mathrm{TAS}$ concentrates near $\sqrt{d/n}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration},
and this is the chance level against which any reported alignment must be
judged. The sheaf coherence error is the mean disagreement on overlaps,
$E_{\mathrm{sheaf}}$ of Section~\ref{sec:coherence}. Effective rank of a
Jacobian is the participation ratio $(\sum\sigma_i)^2/\sum\sigma_i^2$ of its
singular values.

\paragraph{Synthetic manifolds.} Tangent spaces are known exactly for the
following families: spheres $S^d$, tori, and the Swiss roll, each embedded in
$\R^n$ by a random isometric map so that the ambient dimension $n$ and the
intrinsic dimension $d$ can be varied independently; cones and the Whitney
umbrella supply stratified cases with known singular strata.

\begin{description}
\item[H1 (rank gap).] Train three denoisers on the same data, a direct-output
clean predictor, a direct-output noise predictor, and a skip-connected noise
predictor that computes $(y-af_x(y))/b$ from the clean network. At points on the
scaled manifold and a range of noise levels $b$ and widths, estimate the scaled
Jacobians $a\,Df_x$ and $b\,Df_\varepsilon$ and their participation ratios.
Propositions~\ref{prop:rankgap} and~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit} predict limits $d$
and $n-d$ for the exact optimal predictors as $b\to0$, and Example~\ref{ex:affine}
gives an exactly solvable calibration case. The null is equal participation
ratios for the two direct-output models. The skip-connected control tests how
much of any advantage of clean prediction comes from the requirement that the
learned path carry the near-identity normal response. The prediction should
degrade as $b\sqrt n/\tau$ grows.
\item[H2 (alignment above chance).] For each trained model, report $\mathrm{TAS}$
of the learned update field and of a field built by randomly rotating it,
with the baseline $\sqrt{d/n}$ and confidence intervals over seeds.
\item[H3 (coupling and tangency).] On a Transformer trained on the synthetic
data and on a public image model, estimate local tangent spaces of
residual-stream activations by local PCA and compute principal angles to the
row and column spaces of each head's coupling matrix $B$.
Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention} predicts angles significantly below the chance
value. The null is alignment at chance, and a negative result is informative.
\item[H4 (calibrated test for residual structure).] After fitting a
$d$-dimensional manifold, express the residuals in a common frame and compare
the leading eigenvalues of their covariance with a null distribution obtained
by parametric bootstrap that reproduces the whole fitting and projection
procedure, using the Marchenko--Pastur edge only as a reference. The null is
isotropic residuals. Run the test on data with a planted extra direction of
known strength to measure power, and on data with anisotropic measurement error
and uncorrected curvature to measure the rate of false attribution to an
omitted tangent direction (Definition~\ref{def:structure}).
\end{description}

\paragraph{Reporting.} The number of seeds, sample sizes, the estimator for
tangent spaces and its neighborhood radius, and the estimator for intrinsic
dimension~\cite{levina2005maximum} should be fixed before running, and
negative results reported with the same prominence as positive ones.

\section{Limitations}

Estimating $M$ from finite samples introduces bias that propagates into
tangent estimates, curvature and normal directions, and the achievable rates
depend on the dimension, the reach and the smoothness~\cite{aamari2019nonasymptotic}. The
smoothness assumption may fail for real data, which can be non-embedded, can
contain singularities and can change topology over time, so a stratified
treatment is needed. The rank-gap propositions describe scaled Jacobians at points of the scaled manifold in the limit $b\to0$, and they are not uniform in $b\sqrt n/\tau$ or valid at large noise levels. Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention} is untested here.
The sheaf-theoretic formulation requires a specification of the context
site, and a wrong site produces spurious defects. The interpretive extensions
of Section~\ref{sec:applications} are proposals about description and carry
no empirical support from this paper. The code in the appendix has not been
run against the protocol above.

\section{Future Work}\label{sec:future}

Natural directions include dynamic manifolds $M(t)$ with time-dependent
embeddings, manifolds given as unions $\bigcup_iM_i$ with tangent-constrained
mixture flows, and persistent homology for detecting topological change in
evolving representations. On the theoretical side, the quantitative control of
the posterior mean beyond the leading order in $b/\tau$, and a proof or
refutation of Conjecture~\ref{conj:attention} for a tractable class of
attention models, would convert the two main open claims into results. The
divergence measure $\delta$ of Section~\ref{sec:applications} could be
operationalized for a concrete institutional proxy, and the code-length
criterion of Definition~\ref{def:structure} could be applied to a corpus of
verified exceptions to measure how often they were found to expose a missing
hypothesis and how often they were absorbed as noise.

\section*{Conclusion}

Reality is taken here to have lawful structure that a system can track or
fail to track. The framework proposes that the tracking be understood
geometrically: an update that keeps the data manifold invariant is tangent
(Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}), noise is almost entirely normal
(Lemma~\ref{lem:concentration}), and the scaled Jacobians of the optimal clean and noise predictors converge to the tangent and normal halves of the identity (Propositions~\ref{prop:rankgap} and~\ref{prop:rankgaplimit}).
The word noise, however, is never absolute. A residual is noise relative to a claim and a model class. A verified counterexample refutes a universal claim deductively, a statistical exception rejects a noise model only at a stated level, and a single case has weight $1/N$ in a fixed empirical average although it can move a refitted model further when it introduces a new regime. The question to press
in each case is what is being called noise and with respect to which claim,
and whether the exception exposes a missing hypothesis or a family of cases
that the original formulation concealed. The thesis that intelligence is
persistent admissibility under transformation is offered as the organizing
principle of the framework and as a hypothesis to be tested by the
protocol of Section~\ref{sec:protocol}, not as a consequence of the results proved
here.

\appendix

\section{Background}\label{app:background}

\paragraph{Reach and tubular neighborhoods.} For a compact $C^2$ submanifold the
reach is positive, the nearest-point map $\Pi_M$ is $C^1$ on the open tube of
radius $\reach(M)$ around $M$, and every point of the tube has a unique
nearest point in $M$~\cite{federer1959curvature,lee2013smooth}.

\paragraph{Whitney stratification.} A Whitney stratification of a closed set
$X\subset\R^n$ is a locally finite partition into smooth submanifolds (strata)
satisfying the frontier condition, in which the closure of a stratum is a union
of strata, and Whitney's conditions A and B, which control the limits of
tangent spaces and secant lines at points of lower strata. Every
semialgebraic set admits such a stratification, and stratified Morse
theory extends gradient-flow arguments to these spaces~\cite{goresky1988stratified}. The
framework assumes a stratified structure whenever data are not globally smooth,
and in the setting of Section~\ref{sec:claim} the strata are the natural
carriers of exceptions.

\paragraph{Morse functions.} A smooth function $U$ is Morse if every critical
point is nondegenerate. Morse functions are generic in the $C^2$ topology, and
near a nondegenerate critical point of index $\lambda$ there are coordinates in
which $U$ is a quadratic form with $\lambda$ negative squares~\cite{milnor1963morse}.

\section{Computational Methods}\label{app:methods}

\paragraph{Tangent estimation.} Given samples $\{x_i\}$ from $M$, collect the
neighbors of $x_i$ within radius $r$, form the covariance
$\Sigma_i=k^{-1}\sum_{j}(x_j-x_i)(x_j-x_i)^\top$, and take the top $d$ eigenvectors
as an estimate of $T_{x_i}M$. The angular error grows with the ratio of $r$ to
the reach and with the noise level~\cite{singer2012vector}, so $r$ must be
chosen from the data, and no fixed rate is assumed here.

\paragraph{Chart learning and tangent bases.} When a decoder $G$ is available,
the tangent space at $G(z)$ is the range of the Jacobian $DG(z)$, and an
orthonormal basis is obtained from its singular value decomposition. The
Jacobian must be that of the vector-valued map $G$, and not that of a scalar
reduction of it.

\paragraph{Projection.} The nearest-point projection onto $G(\R^k)$ is computed
by Gauss--Newton iteration in latent coordinates,
$z\leftarrow z-DG(z)^{+}\bigl(G(z)-x\bigr)$, with the pseudoinverse supplied
by a least-squares solve.

\paragraph{Intrinsic dimension.} The maximum-likelihood estimator of Levina and
Bickel is
\[
\hat d=\Bigl[\tfrac1{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\log\tfrac{r_k}{r_i}\Bigr]^{-1},
\]
with $r_i$ the distance to the $i$th neighbor~\cite{levina2005maximum}.

\paragraph{Reference code.} The following unbatched sketch implements the
tangent basis, the retraction, one tangent step of a CLIO loop, and the
alignment diagnostics. It uses the clean-data denoising objective for the
chart.

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python,caption={Tangent step, retraction and diagnostics (unbatched).}]
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.func import jacrev

class Decoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, k, n, hidden=256):
        super().__init__()
        self.net = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(k, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, n))
    def forward(self, z):
        return self.net(z)

class Encoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, n, k, hidden=256):
        super().__init__()
        self.net = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(n, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, hidden), nn.SiLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden, k))
    def forward(self, x):
        return self.net(x)

def tangent_basis(dec, z, d):
    # Jacobian of the vector-valued decoder, shape (n, k)
    J = jacrev(dec)(z)
    U, S, _ = torch.linalg.svd(J, full_matrices=False)
    return U[:, :d]

def project_tangent(U, v):
    return U @ (U.T @ v)

def gauss_newton_project(dec, x, z0, iters=5):
    z = z0.clone()
    for _ in range(iters):
        J = jacrev(dec)(z)
        r = (dec(z) - x).unsqueeze(-1)
        z = z - torch.linalg.lstsq(J, r).solution.squeeze(-1)
    return z

def clio_step(dec, enc, grad_potential, x, d, eta):
    z = enc(x)
    U = tangent_basis(dec, z, d)
    v = -eta * project_tangent(U, grad_potential(x))
    z_new = gauss_newton_project(dec, x + v, z)
    return dec(z_new)

def tas_nmc(U, v):
    v_t = U @ (U.T @ v)
    nv = v.norm()
    return v_t.norm() / nv, (v - v_t).norm() / nv

def denoising_step(enc, dec, opt, x, sigma):
    # clean-data target: predict x from the noised input
    xt = x + sigma * torch.randn_like(x)
    loss = ((dec(enc(xt)) - x) ** 2).mean()
    opt.zero_grad()
    loss.backward()
    opt.step()
    return loss.item()
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Failure Cases and Boundary Conditions}\label{app:failure}

Tangent estimation degrades when the curvature is large relative to the
neighborhood radius, and the nearest-point projection is not unique beyond
the reach. At points of lower strata, such as cusps, the gradient flow is
ill posed because the tangent space is not defined, and trajectories can
jump between components of a disconnected $M$ if the transitions are not
modeled. A wrong estimate of the global topology invalidates the projection
and the potential. A near rank-deficient Jacobian $DG(z)$ makes the
Gauss--Newton projection unstable. A mis-specified context site produces
spurious gluing defects. Each case is a place where the invariance criterion
loses its meaning because the objects it refers to are not well defined.
